People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, hi Doc. Nice to meet you. Thanks for fitting me in. Honestly, I don't even know exactly where to start with all this, but I guess... well, I’m here because I just feel like I’m always running hot. You know what I mean? Like an engine that's just idling way, way too high and I can’t—I literally can’t—shut the motor off. It’s just humming all the time, right in my chest, just this constant buzzing feeling. 

And look, I’m a firefighter, right? So having a lot of energy, being ready to go from zero to sixty in the middle of the night, that’s usually a good thing. When the tones drop, you want a guy who's ready to jump on the rig. But the problem is the in-between times. We have a lot of downtime at the station, and you're supposed to rest, reset, maybe sit in the recliners or cook with the guys. But I can't do the downtime. I just can't do it. I'm always pacing around the bay, or reorganizing the compartments on the truck for the fifth time, or just... vibrating. And lately, man, that’s been getting me in real trouble at the firehouse. The captain actually pulled me aside because I'm just driving everyone crazy, and I guess I'm driving myself crazy too. It's just this non-stop, 24/7 motor, and I don't know how to pull the plug on it anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sudden? No, no, definitely not sudden. I wish it was, honestly, because then maybe there'd be an easy fix, like I just drank too much coffee or something, you know? But no, if you ask my mom... man, she’d probably just laugh and roll her eyes if you asked if this was a new thing. She actually reminded me the other day when I told her I was finally coming to talk to somebody. She says I was exactly like this way back in grade school. Like, the exact same motor running all the time. 

She was telling me how I was just always, always out of my seat. It didn't matter where they put my desk—front of the room, back of the room—I’d be up sharpening my pencil for the fourth time, or going to the trash can, or just wandering around looking at whatever was on the walls. And the blurting out... man, I just couldn't hold it in. The teacher would ask a question and I’d just shout the answer before anyone else could even get their hand halfway up. It wasn't that I was trying to be disrespectful or a bad kid, the thought was just in my head and it had to come out right that exact second. 

And my mom used to just lose her mind over my homework, because I’d actually do it! Like, I was bright enough to do the work, the teachers always told her I was smart and understood the material perfectly fine, but I’d just lose the actual physical piece of paper between the kitchen table and my backpack. It was like a black hole. So my mom was constantly getting these notes sent home from my teachers. Just a never-ending stream of notes saying I wouldn't stay seated, or I was interrupting the class, or I lost my math worksheet again. So, yeah... this has been my whole life, basically. It's just now, as a 37-year-old guy, it's a lot less cute than it was when I was eight, and I'm supposed to be able to just sit in a chair at the firehouse and chill out like a normal adult.

And during those times, did you need less sleep than normal but still feel great?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / firefighter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusually happy? Or like, confident? I mean... I wouldn't say unusually happy, no. I'm just a regular guy, you know? I have good days and bad days like anybody else. I mean, sure, if we get a good call, like a real working fire, and we get in there and do our job right, yeah, I feel great. I feel pumped up. The adrenaline is going. But that's just the job, that's not like... I'm not walking around feeling like Superman for no reason. 

And as far as the racing thoughts... I mean, my brain is always going. Always. But it's not like it's going fast because I'm super excited or something. It's just... it's just cluttered. Like there's ten TVs on in my head all on different channels, and I'm trying to watch all of them at once. It's not this grand, amazing feeling where I'm coming up with brilliant ideas a mile a minute. It's more like, "Did I check the oil on the rig? What's for dinner? I need to text my sister back. Why is that guy looking at me? I should reorganize my locker." Just a constant stream of junk, really. 

And the sleep thing... man, I wish I felt great on less sleep. The guys at the station, they can sleep through anything if it's not our tone. But me? I'm a terrible sleeper. Even when I'm exhausted, I'm just lying there, tossing and turning, my legs are twitching, my brain is doing that ten-TV thing. But I don't feel good about it. I don't wake up the next day feeling like a million bucks because I only slept three hours. I feel like garbage. I'm dragging, I'm irritable, I'm drinking my weight in coffee just to function. So, no, I've never had a time where I just didn't need sleep and felt amazing. I always need sleep, I just can't always get it because I can't shut off. So yeah, I don't think I've ever had what you're talking about. It's just the same old annoying buzzing all the time, not some kind of super-mood.

  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi Doc. Nice to meet you. Thanks for fitting me in. Honestly, I don't even know exactly where to start with all this, but I guess... well, I’m here because I just feel like I’m always running hot. You know what I mean? Like an engine that's just idling way, way too high and I can’t—I literally can’t—shut the motor off. It’s just humming all the time, right in my chest, just this constant buzzing feeling. \n\nAnd look, I’m a firefighter, right? So having a lot of energy, being ready to go from zero to sixty in the middle of the night, that’s usually a good thing. When the tones drop, you want a guy who's ready to jump on the rig. But the problem is the in-between times. We have a lot of downtime at the station, and you're supposed to rest, reset, maybe sit in the recliners or cook with the guys. But I can't do the downtime. I just can't do it. I'm always pacing around the bay, or reorganizing the compartments on the truck for the fifth time, or just... vibrating. And lately, man, that’s been getting me in real trouble at the firehouse. The captain actually pulled me aside because I'm just driving everyone crazy, and I guess I'm driving myself crazy too. It's just this non-stop, 24/7 motor, and I don't know how to pull the plug on it anymore.\n\nDoctor: When did this constant buzzing feeling and inability to sit still start? Was it something that came on suddenly over days or weeks, or has it been pretty much your whole life?\n\nPatient: Sudden? No, no, definitely not sudden. I wish it was, honestly, because then maybe there'd be an easy fix, like I just drank too much coffee or something, you know? But no, if you ask my mom... man, she’d probably just laugh and roll her eyes if you asked if this was a new thing. She actually reminded me the other day when I told her I was finally coming to talk to somebody. She says I was exactly like this way back in grade school. Like, the exact same motor running all the time. \n\nShe was telling me how I was just always, always out of my seat. It didn't matter where they put my desk—front of the room, back of the room—I’d be up sharpening my pencil for the fourth time, or going to the trash can, or just wandering around looking at whatever was on the walls. And the blurting out... man, I just couldn't hold it in. The teacher would ask a question and I’d just shout the answer before anyone else could even get their hand halfway up. It wasn't that I was trying to be disrespectful or a bad kid, the thought was just in my head and it had to come out right that exact second. \n\nAnd my mom used to just lose her mind over my homework, because I’d actually do it! Like, I was bright enough to do the work, the teachers always told her I was smart and understood the material perfectly fine, but I’d just lose the actual physical piece of paper between the kitchen table and my backpack. It was like a black hole. So my mom was constantly getting these notes sent home from my teachers. Just a never-ending stream of notes saying I wouldn't stay seated, or I was interrupting the class, or I lost my math worksheet again. So, yeah... this has been my whole life, basically. It's just now, as a 37-year-old guy, it's a lot less cute than it was when I was eight, and I'm supposed to be able to just sit in a chair at the firehouse and chill out like a normal adult.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness and constant energy, have you ever had periods where you felt unusually happy, confident, or like your thoughts were racing really fast—like your mind was going a mile a minute? And during those times, did you need less sleep than normal but still feel great?\n\nPatient: Unusually happy? Or like, confident? I mean... I wouldn't say unusually happy, no. I'm just a regular guy, you know? I have good days and bad days like anybody else. I mean, sure, if we get a good call, like a real working fire, and we get in there and do our job right, yeah, I feel great. I feel pumped up. The adrenaline is going. But that's just the job, that's not like... I'm not walking around feeling like Superman for no reason. \n\nAnd as far as the racing thoughts... I mean, my brain is always going. Always. But it's not like it's going fast because I'm super excited or something. It's just... it's just cluttered. Like there's ten TVs on in my head all on different channels, and I'm trying to watch all of them at once. It's not this grand, amazing feeling where I'm coming up with brilliant ideas a mile a minute. It's more like, \"Did I check the oil on the rig? What's for dinner? I need to text my sister back. Why is that guy looking at me? I should reorganize my locker.\" Just a constant stream of junk, really. \n\nAnd the sleep thing... man, I wish I felt great on less sleep. The guys at the station, they can sleep through anything if it's not our tone. But me? I'm a terrible sleeper. Even when I'm exhausted, I'm just lying there, tossing and turning, my legs are twitching, my brain is doing that ten-TV thing. But I don't feel good about it. I don't wake up the next day feeling like a million bucks because I only slept three hours. I feel like garbage. I'm dragging, I'm irritable, I'm drinking my weight in coffee just to function. So, no, I've never had a time where I just didn't need sleep and felt amazing. I always need sleep, I just can't always get it because I can't shut off. So yeah, I don't think I've ever had what you're talking about. It's just the same old annoying buzzing all the time, not some kind of super-mood.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned losing your homework as a kid and losing papers. Do you still have that problem now as an adult—like, do you frequently lose things, forget where you put stuff, or have trouble keeping track of important items or paperwork?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / firefighter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Actually, no. No, I really don't. Which is kind of funny when you think about it, considering how bad I was as a kid with the homework, right? But no, as an adult... I don't really lose my stuff at all. I don't know if I just outgrew that one specific thing or if it's because of the job, but I actually know exactly where all my things are all the time. 

I mean, especially at the firehouse, you kind of have to, right? It's literally life or death. When the tones drop at 3 AM, you can't be wandering around the bay looking for your gloves. So my gear is always, always staged perfectly. When I get in at 0700 for my shift, the very first thing I do is set up my turnout gear. My boots are already stepped into my bunker pants right next to the jump seat, my Nomex hood is tucked exactly inside my helmet, and my helmet is sitting right on the seat. And because I'm always pacing around—like I was telling you before, how I can't just sit in the recliner and watch TV with the guys—I end up checking my gear over and over. I'll walk by the rig and just pat my pockets. Phone, wallet, keys. Phone, wallet, keys. Just tapping them constantly as I walk around. 

And as far as paperwork goes, we have to do run reports after every single medical call or fire, and I never lose those. Granted, most of it is on the Toughbook laptop in the engine now, so it's hard to physically lose a heavy computer that's bolted to the dashboard, but even with the physical papers, like the patient refusal forms or whatever, I never lose them. I just clip them right to the clipboard immediately. My locker is the exact same way, everything is lined up perfectly. Honestly, if one of the other guys reorganizes a compartment on the truck and shifts the Halligan bar or the flathead axe even an inch, I notice it instantly. I'll go right in and move it back. 

So yeah, I really don't have any issue keeping track of my stuff or forgetting where I put things nowadays. It's really just that internal motor, that physical buzzing thing that's driving me and everyone else crazy, not losing my keys or anything like that. I've got my stuff locked down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Man, I wish it was just me being annoying. Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. 

But... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. 

A couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. And it wasn't just him, the battalion chief was sitting in there too. Which, you know, when the white shirts get involved, your stomach just drops to the floor. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. 

They basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. 

And the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. 

So, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. They were completely right.

She actually reminded me the other day when I told her I was finally coming to talk to somebody. She says I was exactly like this way back in grade school. Like, the exact same motor running all the time. \n\nShe was telling me how I was just always, always out of my seat. It didn't matter where they put my desk—front of the room, back of the room—I’d be up sharpening my pencil for the fourth time, or going to the trash can, or just wandering around looking at whatever was on the walls. And the blurting out... man, I just couldn't hold it in. The teacher would ask a question and I’d just shout the answer before anyone else could even get their hand halfway up. It wasn't that I was trying to be disrespectful or a bad kid, the thought was just in my head and it had to come out right that exact second. \n\nAnd my mom used to just lose her mind over my homework, because I’d actually do it! Like, I was bright enough to do the work, the teachers always told her I was smart and understood the material perfectly fine, but I’d just lose the actual physical piece of paper between the kitchen table and my backpack. It was like a black hole. So my mom was constantly getting these notes sent home from my teachers. Just a never-ending stream of notes saying I wouldn't stay seated, or I was interrupting the class, or I lost my math worksheet again. So, yeah... this has been my whole life, basically. It's just now, as a 37-year-old guy, it's a lot less cute than it was when I was eight, and I'm supposed to be able to just sit in a chair at the firehouse and chill out like a normal adult.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness and constant energy, have you ever had periods where you felt unusually happy, confident, or like your thoughts were racing really fast—like your mind was going a mile a minute? And during those times, did you need less sleep than normal but still feel great?\n\nPatient: Unusually happy? Or like, confident? I mean... I wouldn't say unusually happy, no. I'm just a regular guy, you know? I have good days and bad days like anybody else. I mean, sure, if we get a good call, like a real working fire, and we get in there and do our job right, yeah, I feel great. I feel pumped up. The adrenaline is going. But that's just the job, that's not like... I'm not walking around feeling like Superman for no reason. \n\nAnd as far as the racing thoughts... I mean, my brain is always going. Always. But it's not like it's going fast because I'm super excited or something. It's just... it's just cluttered. Like there's ten TVs on in my head all on different channels, and I'm trying to watch all of them at once. It's not this grand, amazing feeling where I'm coming up with brilliant ideas a mile a minute. It's more like, \"Did I check the oil on the rig? What's for dinner? I need to text my sister back. Why is that guy looking at me? I should reorganize my locker.\" Just a constant stream of junk, really. \n\nAnd the sleep thing... man, I wish I felt great on less sleep. The guys at the station, they can sleep through anything if it's not our tone. But me? I'm a terrible sleeper. Even when I'm exhausted, I'm just lying there, tossing and turning, my legs are twitching, my brain is doing that ten-TV thing. But I don't feel good about it. I don't wake up the next day feeling like a million bucks because I only slept three hours. I feel like garbage. I'm dragging, I'm irritable, I'm drinking my weight in coffee just to function. So, no, I've never had a time where I just didn't need sleep and felt amazing. I always need sleep, I just can't always get it because I can't shut off. So yeah, I don't think I've ever had what you're talking about. It's just the same old annoying buzzing all the time, not some kind of super-mood.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing your homework as a kid and losing papers. Do you still have that problem now as an adult—like, do you frequently lose things, forget where you put stuff, or have trouble keeping track of important items or paperwork?\n\nPatient: Actually, no. No, I really don't. Which is kind of funny when you think about it, considering how bad I was as a kid with the homework, right? But no, as an adult... I don't really lose my stuff at all. I don't know if I just outgrew that one specific thing or if it's because of the job, but I actually know exactly where all my things are all the time. \n\nI mean, especially at the firehouse, you kind of have to, right? It's literally life or death. When the tones drop at 3 AM, you can't be wandering around the bay looking for your gloves. So my gear is always, always staged perfectly. When I get in at 0700 for my shift, the very first thing I do is set up my turnout gear. My boots are already stepped into my bunker pants right next to the jump seat, my Nomex hood is tucked exactly inside my helmet, and my helmet is sitting right on the seat. And because I'm always pacing around—like I was telling you before, how I can't just sit in the recliner and watch TV with the guys—I end up checking my gear over and over. I'll walk by the rig and just pat my pockets. Phone, wallet, keys. Phone, wallet, keys. Just tapping them constantly as I walk around. \n\nAnd as far as paperwork goes, we have to do run reports after every single medical call or fire, and I never lose those. Granted, most of it is on the Toughbook laptop in the engine now, so it's hard to physically lose a heavy computer that's bolted to the dashboard, but even with the physical papers, like the patient refusal forms or whatever, I never lose them. I just clip them right to the clipboard immediately. My locker is the exact same way, everything is lined up perfectly. Honestly, if one of the other guys reorganizes a compartment on the truck and shifts the Halligan bar or the flathead axe even an inch, I notice it instantly. I'll go right in and move it back. \n\nSo yeah, I really don't have any issue keeping track of my stuff or forgetting where I put things nowadays. It's really just that internal motor, that physical buzzing thing that's driving me and everyone else crazy, not losing my keys or anything like that. I've got my stuff locked down.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that as a kid you had trouble with homework and your teachers kept sending notes home about not staying seated and interrupting. But you also just told me that now at work, you're organized and don't lose things. Has your job performance or your standing with your captain or coworkers actually suffered because of the restlessness and the inability to sit still, or is it more just that you're annoying people but still getting your work done?\n\nPatient: Man, I wish it was just me being annoying. Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. \n\nBut... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. And it wasn't just him, the battalion chief was sitting in there too. Which, you know, when the white shirts get involved, your stomach just drops to the floor. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. \n\nThey basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. \n\nAnd the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. They were completely right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

  }

No, I really don't. Which is kind of funny when you think about it, considering how bad I was as a kid with the homework, right? But no, as an adult... I don't really lose my stuff at all. I don't know if I just outgrew that one specific thing or if it's because of the job, but I actually know exactly where all my things are all the time. \n\nI mean, especially at the firehouse, you kind of have to, right? It's literally life or death. When the tones drop at 3 AM, you can't be wandering around the bay looking for your gloves. So my gear is always, always staged perfectly. When I get in at 0700 for my shift, the very first thing I do is set up my turnout gear. My boots are already stepped into my bunker pants right next to the jump seat, my Nomex hood is tucked exactly inside my helmet, and my helmet is sitting right on the seat. And because I'm always pacing around—like I was telling you before, how I can't just sit in the recliner and watch TV with the guys—I end up checking my gear over and over. I'll walk by the rig and just pat my pockets. Phone, wallet, keys. Phone, wallet, keys. Just tapping them constantly as I walk around. \n\nAnd as far as paperwork goes, we have to do run reports after every single medical call or fire, and I never lose those. Granted, most of it is on the Toughbook laptop in the engine now, so it's hard to physically lose a heavy computer that's bolted to the dashboard, but even with the physical papers, like the patient refusal forms or whatever, I never lose them. I just clip them right to the clipboard immediately. My locker is the exact same way, everything is lined up perfectly. Honestly, if one of the other guys reorganizes a compartment on the truck and shifts the Halligan bar or the flathead axe even an inch, I notice it instantly. I'll go right in and move it back. \n\nSo yeah, I really don't have any issue keeping track of my stuff or forgetting where I put things nowadays. It's really just that internal motor, that physical buzzing thing that's driving me and everyone else crazy, not losing my keys or anything like that. I've got my stuff locked down.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that as a kid you had trouble with homework and your teachers kept sending notes home about not staying seated and interrupting. But you also just told me that now at work, you're organized and don't lose things. Has your job performance or your standing with your captain or coworkers actually suffered because of the restlessness and the inability to sit still, or is it more just that you're annoying people but still getting your work done?\n\nPatient: Man, I wish it was just me being annoying. Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. \n\nBut... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. \n\nThey basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. \n\nAnd the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's... that's a good question. It's kind of a mix of both, honestly, but the focus part is definitely a massive issue. It's not just about not wanting to sit still, though, like I said, sitting still is basically torture for me. But even when I force myself to sit there, like, physically plant myself in the chair to study those tactical manuals or do the run reports... my brain just won't cooperate. 

I mean, take the drone training we've been doing lately. The department just got these new thermal imaging drones, right? Really cool stuff, super helpful for finding hot spots on a roof without having to actually put guys up there. So we have these classroom sessions, these briefings where the instructor is going over the flight protocols, the FAA regulations, how to read the thermal signatures on the screen... and I go into it telling myself, "Okay, you need to know this. This is important. Pay attention." 

And for the first few minutes, I'm locked in. I really am. I'm looking at the instructor, I'm nodding, I'm taking it all in. But then... it's like a switch flips, and I don't even realize it's happening. Suddenly, I'll realize I've just been staring at the map on the wall, or staring at the drone controller on the table, for who knows how long. And the instructor is still talking, but it's just Charlie Brown teacher noises in my head. I'll snap back to reality and realize I didn't catch half of what command just said. Like, I completely missed the part about battery swap procedures or whatever. 

It's exactly the same with the tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam. I'll sit down at the kitchen table at the firehouse after dinner, when things are quiet. I'll open the book, read a paragraph, and it makes sense. I'll read the next paragraph, and then... I'm reading the words, my eyes are moving across the page, but nothing is going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. I was thinking about whether the pump on Engine 4 sounded a little rough earlier, or if I need to get my boots resoled, or what the guy on the B-shift said to me yesterday. So I have to go back and read the same page over again. And sometimes I have to read it three or four times before the information actually sticks. 

It's so frustrating because I *want* to learn it. I'm not bored by it. I love this job, I love the tactics, I want to be a lieutenant. But it's like trying to hold water in my hands. No matter how hard I squeeze, it just slips right through my fingers. And it takes me twice as long to get through a chapter as it does anyone else, and even then, I'm not confident I actually retained it. So yeah, it's definitely a struggle to actually concentrate and stick with it, even when I'm trying my absolute hardest.

Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. \n\nBut... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. And it wasn't just him, the battalion chief was sitting in there too. Which, you know, when the white shirts get involved, your stomach just drops to the floor. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. \n\nThey basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. \n\nAnd the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. They were completely right.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your brain feels like ten TVs on different channels all at once, and that you jump around on scenes instead of doing systematic step-by-step procedures. But let me ask more specifically: when you're trying to do something that requires you to focus for a while—like reading those tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam, or filling out run reports—do you find it hard to actually concentrate and stick with it, or do you just not want to sit still while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a good question. It's kind of a mix of both, honestly, but the focus part is definitely a massive issue. It's not just about not wanting to sit still, though, like I said, sitting still is basically torture for me. But even when I force myself to sit there, like, physically plant myself in the chair to study those tactical manuals or do the run reports... my brain just won't cooperate. \n\nI mean, take the drone training we've been doing lately. The department just got these new thermal imaging drones, right? Really cool stuff, super helpful for finding hot spots on a roof without having to actually put guys up there. So we have these classroom sessions, these briefings where the instructor is going over the flight protocols, the FAA regulations, how to read the thermal signatures on the screen... and I go into it telling myself, \"Okay, you need to know this. This is important. Pay attention.\" \n\nAnd for the first few minutes, I'm locked in. I really am. I'm looking at the instructor, I'm nodding, I'm taking it all in. But then... it's like a switch flips, and I don't even realize it's happening. Suddenly, I'll realize I've just been staring at the map on the wall, or staring at the drone controller on the table, for who knows how long. And the instructor is still talking, but it's just Charlie Brown teacher noises in my head. I'll snap back to reality and realize I didn't catch half of what command just said. Like, I completely missed the part about battery swap procedures or whatever. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam. I'll sit down at the kitchen table at the firehouse after dinner, when things are quiet. I'll open the book, read a paragraph, and it makes sense. I'll read the next paragraph, and then... I'm reading the words, my eyes are moving across the page, but nothing is going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. I was thinking about whether the pump on Engine 4 sounded a little rough earlier, or if I need to get my boots resoled, or what the guy on the B-shift said to me yesterday. So I have to go back and read the same page over again. And sometimes I have to read it three or four times before the information actually sticks. \n\nIt's so frustrating because I *want* to learn it. I'm not bored by it. I love this job, I love the tactics, I want to be a lieutenant. But it's like trying to hold water in my hands. No matter how hard I squeeze, it just slips right through my fingers. And it takes me twice as long to get through a chapter as it does anyone else, and even then, I'm not confident I actually retained it. I've got my stuff locked down.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that as a kid you had trouble with homework and your teachers kept sending notes home about not staying seated and interrupting. But you also just told me that now at work, you're organized and don't lose things. Has your job performance or your standing with your captain or coworkers actually suffered because of the restlessness and the inability to sit still, or is it more just that you're annoying people but still getting your work done?\n\nPatient: Man, I wish it was just me being annoying. Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. \n\nBut... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. And it wasn't just him, the battalion chief was sitting in there too. Which, you know, when the white shirts get involved, your stomach just drops to the floor. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. \n\nThey basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. \n\nAnd the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. They were completely right.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your brain feels like ten TVs on different channels all at once, and that you jump around on scenes instead of doing systematic step-by-step procedures. But let me ask more specifically: when you're trying to do something that requires you to focus for a while—like reading those tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam, or filling out run reports—do you find it hard to actually concentrate and stick with it, or do you just not want to sit still while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a good question. It's kind of a mix of both, honestly, but the focus part is definitely a massive issue. It's not just about not wanting to sit still, though, like I said, sitting still is basically torture for me. But even when I force myself to sit there, like, physically plant myself in the chair to study those tactical manuals or do the run reports... my brain just won't cooperate. \n\nI mean, take the drone training we've been doing lately. The department just got these new thermal imaging drones, right? Really cool stuff, super helpful for finding hot spots on a roof without having to actually put guys up there. So we have these classroom sessions, these briefings where the instructor is going over the flight protocols, the FAA regulations, how to read the thermal signatures on the screen... and I go into it telling myself, \"Okay, you need to know this. This is important. Pay attention.\" \n\nAnd for the first few minutes, I'm locked in. I really am. I'm looking at the instructor, I'm nodding, I'm taking it all in. But then... it's like a switch flips, and I don't even realize it's happening. Suddenly, I'll realize I've just been staring at the map on the wall, or staring at the drone controller on the table, for who knows how long. And the instructor is still talking, but it's just Charlie Brown teacher noises in my head. I'll snap back to reality and realize I didn't catch half of what command just said. Like, I completely missed the part about battery swap procedures or whatever. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam. I'll sit down at the kitchen table at the firehouse after dinner, when things are quiet. I'll open the book, read a paragraph, and it makes sense. I'll read the next paragraph, and then... I'm reading the words, my eyes are moving across the page, but nothing is going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. I was thinking about whether the pump on Engine 4 sounded a little rough earlier, or if I need to get my boots resoled, or what the guy on the B-shift said to me yesterday. So I have to go back and read the same page over again. I love this job, I love the tactics, I want to be a lieutenant. But it's like trying to hold water in my hands. No matter how hard I squeeze, it just slips right through my fingers. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. Honestly, the planning part... that's actually something I'm weirdly good at. I don't really struggle to break things down into steps or make a schedule at all. 

Like, if you look at my study plan for the lieutenant's exam—because I really did try to approach it like a professional, you know? I didn't just buy the books and wing it. I went out and got these highlighters, I got a planner, and I sat down and mapped out the entire 600-page tactical manual. I broke it down by week, by day, by chapter. I calculated exactly how many pages I needed to read per shift, factoring in our average call volume, just to make sure I'd be ready by the test date in November. 

So, making the plan? Breaking a big goal into little steps? I can do that all day. It's almost like a puzzle to me, and I like solving it. I do the exact same thing at the firehouse when we have a big project. Like... last month, we hosted this massive regional vehicle extrication drill. We had crews coming from four different surrounding counties. Captain Miller actually put me in charge of the logistics for it, which was a huge deal for me, especially after that whole performance meeting we had. And I totally crushed the planning part. I had a spreadsheet, I had the timeline for when the junk cars were being delivered from the salvage yard, I had the meal schedules figured out down to the minute, I knew exactly which engine company was rotating to which cutting station at what time. I had it all written out on the big whiteboard in the apparatus bay, color-coded and everything. I even planned out the cleanup phases. 

So I don't jump around when I'm *making* the plan. The steps make perfect sense in my head. I can see the whole project from start to finish, and I know exactly what needs to happen first, second, and third. It's just... it's the actual execution part where things get messy for me, like I was saying before with the reading. I can schedule myself to read Chapter 4 on Tuesday at 1900 hours, and I will sit down at exactly 1900 hours to do it, right on schedule... but then my brain just checks out on the words. 

But the actual organizing of the project? Figuring out the steps and setting up a schedule? No, I don't have any issue with that at all. I've always been pretty solid at mapping things out.

It's really just that internal motor, that physical buzzing thing that's driving me and everyone else crazy, not losing my keys or anything like that. But you also just told me that now at work, you're organized and don't lose things. Has your job performance or your standing with your captain or coworkers actually suffered because of the restlessness and the inability to sit still, or is it more just that you're annoying people but still getting your work done?\n\nPatient: Man, I wish it was just me being annoying. Honestly, I really do. You know, in a firehouse, being annoying is kind of a given. We all annoy each other. You live with these guys a third of your life, you eat together, sleep in the same open dorm, scrub the bays together. If it was just me pacing holes in the rug while they're trying to watch the game, they'd just throw a roll of tape at my head and tell me to go wash the rig or something. I could totally handle that. \n\nBut... no, it's actually gotten way worse than that. It's... it's starting to cost me their trust. And in this job, if your crew doesn't trust you, you've got absolutely nothing. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, my captain—Captain Miller, great guy, I've worked under him for like eight years—he actually pulled me into the office. And it wasn't just him, the battalion chief was sitting in there too. Which, you know, when the white shirts get involved, your stomach just drops to the floor. It was a formal performance and safety meeting. Sitting there across from them... man, I felt exactly like I was back in the principal's office in third grade, just sweating through my uniform shirt, waiting for the hammer to drop. \n\nThey basically laid it out for me. They said that while my gear is always squared away and I'm always first on the truck—like I was telling you earlier—my brain is just bouncing around too much when we're actually operating on a scene. Like, we'll be at a messy motor vehicle accident, and I'm supposed to be doing a systematic scene size-up, checking for hazards, downed power lines, leaking fuel, whatever. But instead of locking in on the step-by-step procedures, I'm jumping from the extrication tools to the patient, to the traffic, and I'm missing the updates coming over the radio from command. I'm just not tracking the critical details consistently. Miller said it's like watching a pinball, and it's making the younger guys nervous because they don't know what I'm going to do next or if I heard the safety benchmarks. \n\nAnd the worst part... the absolute worst part of all of this is that I've been studying for the lieutenant's exam. I've been putting in the time, reading the tactical manuals, taking all the practice tests. I really, really wanted to move up into a leadership role this year. It's been my goal since I got my five-year bugle. But after that meeting? I can feel my shot at moving into leadership just totally slipping away. Miller straight up told me that if the crew can't count on me to stay grounded and track the operational details when the tones drop, he can't in good conscience recommend me for the promotion. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not just me being a nuisance anymore. It's actually blowing up my career. And it just kills me, you know? Because I know the job. I know the tactics. I can pump a fire engine in my sleep, I know the friction loss calculations, I know building construction. But because I can't just slow down and focus on the one thing in front of me without my brain wanting to jump to five other things at the exact same time... I'm losing the respect of my crew. I just keep replaying that meeting in my head, over and over, thinking about what I could have said to defend myself, but the thing is... they were right. They were completely right.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your brain feels like ten TVs on different channels all at once, and that you jump around on scenes instead of doing systematic step-by-step procedures. But let me ask more specifically: when you're trying to do something that requires you to focus for a while—like reading those tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam, or filling out run reports—do you find it hard to actually concentrate and stick with it, or do you just not want to sit still while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a good question. It's kind of a mix of both, honestly, but the focus part is definitely a massive issue. It's not just about not wanting to sit still, though, like I said, sitting still is basically torture for me. But even when I force myself to sit there, like, physically plant myself in the chair to study those tactical manuals or do the run reports... my brain just won't cooperate. \n\nI mean, take the drone training we've been doing lately. The department just got these new thermal imaging drones, right? Really cool stuff, super helpful for finding hot spots on a roof without having to actually put guys up there. So we have these classroom sessions, these briefings where the instructor is going over the flight protocols, the FAA regulations, how to read the thermal signatures on the screen... and I go into it telling myself, \"Okay, you need to know this. This is important. Pay attention.\" \n\nAnd for the first few minutes, I'm locked in. I really am. I'm looking at the instructor, I'm nodding, I'm taking it all in. But then... it's like a switch flips, and I don't even realize it's happening. Suddenly, I'll realize I've just been staring at the map on the wall, or staring at the drone controller on the table, for who knows how long. And the instructor is still talking, but it's just Charlie Brown teacher noises in my head. I'll snap back to reality and realize I didn't catch half of what command just said. Like, I completely missed the part about battery swap procedures or whatever. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the tactical manuals for the lieutenant's exam. I'll sit down at the kitchen table at the firehouse after dinner, when things are quiet. I'll open the book, read a paragraph, and it makes sense. I'll read the next paragraph, and then... I'm reading the words, my eyes are moving across the page, but nothing is going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. I was thinking about whether the pump on Engine 4 sounded a little rough earlier, or if I need to get my boots resoled, or what the guy on the B-shift said to me yesterday. So I have to go back and read the same page over again. And sometimes I have to read it three or four times before the information actually sticks. \n\nIt's so frustrating because I *want* to learn it. I'm not bored by it. I love this job, I love the tactics, I want to be a lieutenant. But it's like trying to hold water in my hands. No matter how hard I squeeze, it just slips right through my fingers. And it takes me twice as long to get through a chapter as it does anyone else, and even then, I'm not confident I actually retained it. So yeah, it's definitely a struggle to actually concentrate and stick with it, even when I'm trying my absolute hardest.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something like studying for the lieutenant's exam or doing other tasks that require planning ahead—like organizing a project or managing your schedule—do you find it hard to break things down into steps, or do you tend to jump around and struggle to stick to a plan?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Honestly, the planning part... that's actually something I'm weirdly good at. I don't really struggle to break things down into steps or make a schedule at all. \n\nLike, if you look at my study plan for the lieutenant's exam—because I really did try to approach it like a professional, you know? I didn't just buy the books and wing it. I went out and got these highlighters, I got a planner, and I sat down and mapped out the entire 600-page tactical manual. I broke it down by week, by day, by chapter. I calculated exactly how many pages I needed to read per shift, factoring in our average call volume, just to make sure I'd be ready by the test date in November. \n\nSo, making the plan? Breaking a big goal into little steps? I can do that all day. It's almost like a puzzle to me, and I like solving it. I do the exact same thing at the firehouse when we have a big project. Like... last month, we hosted this massive regional vehicle extrication drill. We had crews coming from four different surrounding counties. Captain Miller actually put me in charge of the logistics for it, which was a huge deal for me, especially after that whole performance meeting we had. And I totally crushed the planning part. I had a spreadsheet, I had the timeline for when the junk cars were being delivered from the salvage yard, I had the meal schedules figured out down to the minute, I knew exactly which engine company was rotating to which cutting station at what time. I had it all written out on the big whiteboard in the apparatus bay, color-coded and everything. I even planned out the cleanup phases. \n\nSo I don't jump around when I'm *making* the plan. The steps make perfect sense in my head. I can see the whole project from start to finish, and I know exactly what needs to happen first, second, and third. It's just... it's the actual execution part where things get messy for me, like I was saying before with the reading. I can schedule myself to read Chapter 4 on Tuesday at 1900 hours, and I will sit down at exactly 1900 hours to do it, right on schedule... but then my brain just checks out on the words. \n\nBut the actual organizing of the project? Figuring out the steps and setting up a schedule? No, I don't have any issue with that at all. I've always been pretty solid at mapping things out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I keep forgetting stuff I honestly mean to do, like signing off on training modules, calling people back, paying a bill before the late fee, or bringing the right gear paperwork in on shift.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: When I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg is bouncing, I’m clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, or messing with whatever is in front of me without even noticing it.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate.
